I haven't really noticed a pattern with my mood triggering it. It just sort of happens... I either don't think about food at all, or I can't stop eating. I haven't really connected it to feeling anxious or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after eating, especially after those late-night episodes of consuming a lot of food?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't really noticed a pattern with my mood triggering it. It just sort of happens... I either don't think about food at all, or I can't stop eating. I haven't really connected it to feeling anxious or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after eating, especially after those late-night episodes of consuming a lot of food?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don't sit there beating myself up over the calories or feeling bad about myself after it happens. It's more just... I guess I just realize it's weird that my appetite is doing this out of nowhere. But I wouldn't say I feel guilty after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
